## AI Research

### ["Contagious Humming" to Silence a Room](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nfcbMeLmSy7LNegoD/contagious-humming-to-silence-a-room)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Often when running meetups you’ll have several lively conversations going at the same time. This is a great problem to have, but it can make it difficult to get everyone’s attention for announcements.</span></p><p><span>Try using “Contagious Humming” when you need to silence a crowd:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Move to a prominent place in the room and use body language that says you have something to say. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Start humming at a low and constant tone.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Recruit a few compatriots to join in. </span><ul><li value="1"><span>If the crowd has enough people familiar with the technique, generally about 20% of the room, looking expectantly at a few friends is often enough. </span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Wait for the hum to spread through the room. This can take a while, 30 seconds to a minute, just be patient. </span><ul><li value="1"><span>There will often be one or two holdouts, but they’ll usually finish their statement within about three to five seconds of noticing everyone else is humming. If not, call their name or have someone tap them on their shoulder to get their attention. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Don’t try to start an announcement by talking over a holdout; in my experience that breaks the spell.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Let the hum fill the room for a few seconds after the last person stops talking. Then stop, take a breath, and thank the crowd. If you’ve done this right, they’ll see you preparing to speak and stop humming. </span></li><li value="6"><span>Say what you came to say.</span></li></ul><p><span>Introducing this technique to a new group is often pretty easy. It helps to get buy-in from some deputies. Explain to a few social-looking people that you want them to call the room to order by getting everyone to hum, aside, then have them circulate through the room to pass the word while you and your starting conversational circle all hum. It’ll take a little longer but, in my experience, people catch on surprisingly quickly.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>This can also be used to reset everyone’s volume. Meetups are often held in rooms with bare walls or floors, causing a subtle echo. Often people instinctually raise their voice to offset this, without even realizing they’re doing it, causing the other conversations in the room to get louder in response. This feedback loop can cause a cacophony that is deeply unpleasant for people with sensory issues, or who have hearing damage making it difficult to separate voices out from background noise.</span></p><p><span>This is a common technique, but it’s barely discussed online.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9thx6lxast"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9thx6lxast">[1]</a></sup></span><span> I didn’t see it documented on LessWrong at all. I wanted this post to exist as a reference that I could link to.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Why does this work?</span></h2><p><span>It’s not rocket science: when you’re humming, you can’t talk. This gradually spreads between conversation circles, as someone in each notices and either drops out of the conversation to hum or starts looking around in confusion to see why everyone has decided to be bees all of a sudden.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdvqkzz4j7dl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndvqkzz4j7dl">[2]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>Humming is effective on holdouts because they notice that more than just their own conversational circle can now hear them. It is inherently awkward to suddenly be talking to a larger crowd who didn’t hear the first part of your point. Often people will try to finish their thought, go on for a few more seconds, and awkwardly trail off as they try to start a new sentence and get confused looks in response. Trust the process, this is fine, they’ll know better next time.</span></p><p><span>Humming is also calming, both to do and to hear. An article in Psychology Today tries to explain why.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhidm269iqpl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhidm269iqpl">[3]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Better Than Alternatives</span></h2><p><span>The fact that you can’t talk while humming makes it better than other methods, like shouting over the crowd, or “clap X times if you can hear me”. Methods focused on sharp sounds like claps or yells can amp up a crowd, making them subtly more likely to keep talking and finishing their point. </span></p><p><span>Yelling or clapping also amps up </span><i><span>you</span></i><span>, the speaker. This is sometimes appropriate, if you’re leading a pep rally, or need the crowd to be energetic for some reasons. But this is best used sparingly, only when you actually intend the effect. Oftentimes you’ll want to calm, dismiss, or give logistical notes to a crowd. In those cases, humming for 30 seconds beforehand is better preparation for your own voice. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Give “Contagious Humming” a try and report back.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9thx6lxast"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9thx6lxast">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This was one of the top search results, and it’s not even the focus of the post: </span><a href="https://experientialtools.com/2012/03/16/large-group-facilitation-tips/"><span>https://experientialtools.com/2012/03/16/large-group-facilitation-tips/</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndvqkzz4j7dl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdvqkzz4j7dl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Their confusion can be very amusing, if you’re into that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhidm269iqpl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhidm269iqpl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Something about the vagus nerve? </span><br /><a href="https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-compassionate-brain/202410/the-power-of-humming"><span>https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-compassionate-brain/202410/the-power-of-humming</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nfcbMeLmSy7LNegoD/contagious-humming-to-silence-a-room#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Dissolving the Deep Learning Sample Efficiency Gap](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tmWxDGnuNdaHFDyjf/dissolving-the-deep-learning-sample-efficiency-gap-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>A common observation about deep learning is that it's wildly sample inefficient compared to humans. Deep learning systems appear to need much more real data or environment interaction to reach a given level of capability. A teenager can learn to drive in a few dozen hours; self-driving systems are trained for years on billions of miles of data. A human can become competitive at StarCraft II in well under a year of play, while AlphaStar required imitation learning from roughly 18 years of human games followed by 13,300 years of self-play to reach Grandmaster</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9lyzj9lpm6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9lyzj9lpm6">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. A 12-year-old has heard perhaps a hundred million words of language; a frontier LLM trains on tens of trillions of tokens. The gap is, on the face of it, enormous.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 57.250755287009056%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/61c7ed92f19313c126cc0011b3e0995274f624cc943b50176079f9e9373cddae/th5s6uyh79davcvib9z8" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>(From </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08165"><span>Warstadt et al. 2025</span></a><span>)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Screenshot from 2026-05-30 16-46-34.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780332581/lexical_client_uploads/cfzabdoptmi4xsxg29by.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>(From </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oxvnREntu82tffkYW/we-need-a-field-of-reward-function-design"><span>Byrnes 2025</span></a><span>)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>What people take this to mean varies widely. Steven Byrnes appears to read the gap as evidence that current algorithms are far from what the brain is doing, such that much better algorithms must be waiting to be found. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yew6zFWAKG4AGs3Wk/foom-and-doom-1-brain-in-a-box-in-a-basement"><span>His guess is that human-level, human-speed AGI will require not a datacenter but "one consumer gaming GPU," even for training from scratch</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc2uzas6fjlg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc2uzas6fjlg">[2]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/oHuyfo8jBpocxKr3D/unsolved-research-problems-on-the-road-to-agi"><span>Yarrow Bouchard on the EA Forum, reads the same gap as evidence that AGI isn't close at all</span></a><span>, precisely because nobody knows how to close it. Nearly opposite conclusions from the same starting observation.</span></p><p><span>In this post I'll argue that both these conclusions are mistaken. Most of the apparent inefficiency dissolves on closer inspection: apples-to-oranges comparisons between pretrained humans and from-scratch networks, hardware and data constraints that push deep learning toward small models trained on enormous corpora, the brain's apparent use of model-based RL of a kind we haven’t yet applied to LLMs, and priors installed by evolution. Real algorithmic gains in sample efficiency are available.&nbsp;But most mechanisms that plausibly&nbsp;close the gap point toward more total training and runtime compute than frontier systems currently use, not less.</span></p><p><span>My best guess is that the gap decomposes into several distinct factors each carrying different explanatory weight depending on the specific comparison at hand.</span></p><h1><b><span>1. All about the priors</span></b></h1><p><span>Sample efficient learning is in large part a property of the representations you arrive with, not of the learning algorithm itself. In Bayesian terms, your representations encode a prior over how the world is structured, and strong priors are what let you reach good posteriors from a handful of observations rather than astronomical amounts of data. Given a sufficiently rich representational substrate, new tasks can often be learned from a few examples. Given a flat prior over a vast hypothesis space, even simple tasks require an enormous amount of data.</span></p><h2><b><span>a. Human priors</span></b></h2><p><span>Most comparisons that yield shocking sample-efficiency ratios between humans and AIs are structurally unfair. They pit a system already shaped by evolution and years of perceptual, motor, causal, social, and linguistic learning against a randomly initialized network that must build many of those representations from scratch while also solving the task we are measuring. Humans don’t learn to drive in thirty hours, they are fine-tuned on driving after a roughly two decade-long pretraining run.</span></p><p><span>One can find evidence for the importance of pre-existing representations in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.10217"><span>Dubey et al. (2018)</span></a><span>. They took a platform-style&nbsp;game environment and deliberately removed cues that humans normally exploit: semantic cues, by rendering meaningful objects as uniform blocks; object/salience cues, by adding many distractor blocks; affordance cues, by filling the background with textures that obscured which surfaces and ladders were usable; similarity cues, by making functionally similar things look visually different; and gravity cues, by rotating the game 90 degrees.&nbsp;Individual ablations substantially slowed human players, and when the main object-related visual cues were masked together, completion time rose from under 2 minutes to over 20. Exploration became close to random, and many players reported falling back on rote memorization.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 96.22356495468279%;"><img alt="combined_first_frame.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780242811/lexical_client_uploads/dmzijjbrzxcfmtuvvfpw.png" /></figure><p><span>When first faced with such a game, a human immediately brings assumptions like: the controllable character is probably the humanoid-looking sprite, gravity points downward, falling off platforms is bad, ladders are for climbing, spikes and monsters are dangerous, and keys open doors. Dubey et al. show that degrading many of these cues makes humans much worse. Importantly, even in the hardest human-tested variants, people were still not reduced to blank-slate RL agents. They retained low-level visual, spatial-navigation, and action-control priors, plus abstract intuitions about object persistence, physics, and causality.</span></p><p><span>Their curiosity-driven RL agent, tested on a smaller related game, was largely unaffected by removing semantic, object, and affordance cues, since it was not exploiting those human priors in the first place, though it was slowed when visual similarity was removed.</span></p><p><span>A similar asymmetry runs&nbsp;through the text-token comparison. A 12-year-old has encountered something like 100 million words</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbg4g66a8xct"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbg4g66a8xct">[3]</a></sup></span><span>, roughly four orders of magnitude below a frontier pretraining corpus. But those words arrive embedded in a continuous multimodal stream: vision, ambient audio, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proprioception"><span>proprioception</span></a><span>, touch, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestibular_system"><span>vestibular signals</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interoception"><span>interoception</span></a><span>. Counted as tokens in the sense a multimodal model would use, the non-text portion of that stream plausibly matches or exceeds frontier corpora (see Appendix). Most of what a word means to the child was learned nonverbally from that stream and then labeled,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm3ome6f3u8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm3ome6f3u8">[4]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;while most of what a token means to a text-only LLM had to be triangulated from textual co-occurrence statistics alone.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmdl00bx003a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmdl00bx003a">[5]</a></sup></span></p><h2><b><span>b. Good representations&nbsp;enable fast learning</span></b></h2><p><span>A frontier LLM, shown a single example of a novel notation, will often pick it up. Shown an unfamiliar API, it can use it correctly after reading the documentation once. Shown a codebase's local conventions, it conforms within a session. The attention mechanism is able to change the activations, layer by layer, until they encode something that solves the new task.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeqbcuzdjhh7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneqbcuzdjhh7">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Given rich enough base representations, the in-context adaptation from a single forward pass can be enough. The</span><a href="https://arcprize.org/leaderboard"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://arcprize.org/leaderboard"><span>ARC-AGI</span></a><span>&nbsp;results of the past two years suggest that as those base representations get richer, the amount of data needed to pin down a novel abstract pattern drops toward something recognizable as human sample efficiency.</span></p><p><span>This is broadly the same story as the child learning a new word. The child has already learned to carve the world into objects, agents, actions, substances, events, and intentions. When they hear "zebra" pointed at a striped horse-shaped thing, the word latches onto a pre-existing slot. Most of the learning happened earlier, across years of pre-linguistic experience. The labeling is cheap because the carving is already done.</span></p><p><span>Deep learning's apparent sample inefficiency is often an artifact of the tabula rasa training regime, not a fundamental property of gradient-based deep learning. "Sample efficiency" is poorly defined without a specification of priors: the same architecture can look astronomically inefficient, trained from scratch, and remarkably efficient adapting from a strong base, for example via in-context learning or using a LoRA.&nbsp;This does not dissolve the whole gap, but it explains many of the most extreme examples. The following sections ask what remains once these comparison artifacts are separated out.</span></p><h1><b><span>2. Model-based RL</span></b></h1><p><span>In addition to the data mix and priors, there are also algorithmic factors that separate current frontier LLMs from the brain. Almost all the compute going into current LLMs is spent on pretraining and on RLVR — reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards in math, code, and similar domains where a correct answer can easily be checked programmatically. What's missing, and what the brain probably leverages in some form</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref64lwn3zjt6f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn64lwn3zjt6f">[7]</a></sup></span><span>, is model-based reinforcement learning: learning a world model that can be used to plan over candidate actions, predict their outcomes, and bootstrap value estimates from imagined trajectories. In any domain where real experiences and reward signals aren't cheap to get, this is the natural mechanism for turning a small number of real interactions into a large amount of learning signal.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefls7nvjol7pc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnls7nvjol7pc">[8]</a></sup></span></p><h2><b><span>a. Dreamer</span></b></h2><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 86.404833836858%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d36a826b2b829a03b37a2e205d7f5446bb878170ac63c4fc674fe72a0f6802db/whqedzbuhs6cmhpcxele" /></figure><p><span>The Dreamer research is probably the cleanest demonstration. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.04104"><span>DreamerV3</span></a><span>&nbsp;(Hafner et al., 2023) trains a recurrent latent world model from raw pixels and vector observations, and uses it to train an actor-critic on trajectories imagined inside that model. The actor is trained to choose actions that score well under these imagined futures, while the critic learns to evaluate returns from both imagined rollouts and replayed experience. With a single fixed set of hyperparameters it matches or beats specialized methods across 150+ tasks, and was the first system to collect diamonds in Minecraft from scratch without human demonstrations or curriculum, a task requiring sparse-reward exploration over thousands of sequential decisions.</span></p><p><span>In their&nbsp;follow up work, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24527"><span>Dreamer 4</span></a><span>&nbsp;(Hafner, Yan &amp; Lillicrap, 2025), they scale this approach to a large transformer-based video world model. It learns a high-resolution Minecraft simulator using </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/vpt/"><span>2.5K-hour VPT contractor dataset</span></a><span>&nbsp;(raw video and mouse/keyboard actions). Leveraging the dataset's event annotations for rewards, the agent improves its task-conditioned policy via reinforcement learning entirely inside imagined rollouts, requiring zero online environment interaction. As a result, Dreamer 4 is the first agent to obtain Minecraft diamonds purely from offline data, substantially outperforming prior VPT and behavioral-cloning baselines which used 100x more data. Additionally, the paper shows that Dreamer 4 does not need action labels for most of its video data. Given all 2.5K hours of Minecraft video but only 100 hours with mouse and keyboard labels, it still learns most of the action-conditioned prediction ability of a fully labeled model, suggesting that future world models could learn broad dynamics from unlabeled video and use smaller paired datasets to ground actions.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.16012084592145%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1f9695dd565d03a6a32f7c43453c98bd9cf75abcd48be9b56d2561b1932d44a8/qwy9nakedyiwqtrorkll" /></figure><p><span>The Dreamer line of work demonstrates that self-supervised world-model training can produce large gains in sample-efficient learning. Once a reusable model of environment dynamics has been learned, downstream RL can extract much more from limited rewards or demonstrations by training on imagined trajectories, rather than requiring new environment interaction for every update. It is thereby possible to learn to play Minecraft entirely offline, without ever directly interacting with the environment.</span></p><h2><span>b. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.00564"><b><span>EfficientZero V2</span></b></a></h2><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 81.87311178247734%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4c4dce900d41abb355d02bc2d4888341d45f9c8d973d311ba65549bbfdabaeeb/enpomicgfyqrhxumbv8j" /></figure><p><span>A complementary line of work is EfficientZero V2 (Wang et al., 2024), which combines learned world models with explicit planning. It extends </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.00210"><span>EfficientZero</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa6h12tylufk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna6h12tylufk">[9]</a></sup></span><span>, a MuZero descendant that learns a latent dynamics model and plans over it with MCTS, to continuous control domains, replacing standard MCTS with a sampling-based </span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=bERaNdoegnO"><span>Gumbel search</span></a><span>&nbsp;using sequential halving. This search procedure samples a finite set of candidate actions and allocates simulations toward the most promising ones, aiming to obtain policy improvement from a small simulation budget.&nbsp;EZ-V2 also re-analyzes old replayed experience with its current model and policy, letting the agent extract fresher learning targets from data collected earlier in training.&nbsp;Thus, it improves sample efficiency by using the world model both to imagine consequences before acting and to get more learning signal out of past interactions.</span></p><p><span>On </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1903.00374"><span>Atari 100k</span></a><span>, which caps the agent at 100,000 environment steps, or 400k Atari frames under action repeat 4, roughly 2 hours of real-time gameplay, EZ-V2 reaches a normalized mean of </span><b><span>2.43</span></b><span>&nbsp;and normalized median of </span><b><span>1.29</span></b><span>, both above the human baseline. The paper thus claims "super-human performance within just 2 hours of real-time gameplay".</span></p><p><span>The result is not uniform across games. EZ-V2 and other strong deep RL agents still struggle badly on some long-horizon, sparse-reward, exploration-heavy games. This pattern is probably best explained with the point&nbsp;in §1 (see also §4b):&nbsp;model-based planning helps extract more from limited experience, but humans bring preexisting semantic and exploration priors, whereas the RL agents have to infer complex game mechanics from scratch under sparse rewards.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5lfh86vv2ja"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5lfh86vv2ja">[10]</a></sup></span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 93.65558912386706%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/616247065fbd39f1cdaf15fbc4b2edb95357f236578ae3abab549b7c71391ef0/qntkisegwyxvmkzwnmwc" /></figure><p><span>LLM post-training pipelines have nothing structurally analogous. RLHF and RLVR are model-free: they update the policy from sampled real trajectories, with no learned model of the environment to plan or imagine inside.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyj6gplx2sll"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyj6gplx2sll">[11]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Whatever fraction of the human–deep-learning gap is genuinely algorithmic rather than an artifact of priors or comparison setup, plausibly shrinks further once frontier systems learn world models over their action space and plan inside them during training and at inference.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span>Some analogous&nbsp;techniques have seen some limited applications during frontier LLM training for narrow domains</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe7yrhxiifoa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne7yrhxiifoa">[12]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;but nobody has demonstrated a general world model rich enough to train and plan inside at the scale and domain-generality LLMs operate in, and AI labs have focused their RL work on areas where the environment and verification step are cheaper to compute directly rather than in a learned world model.</span></p><p><span>A solution to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9rCTjbJpZB4KzqhiQ/you-can-t-imitation-learn-how-to-continual-learn"><span>true continual learning</span></a><span>&nbsp;on harder to simulate and verify domains will likely require both a&nbsp;model-based RL architecture and the kind of context-into-weights compression techniques I described in a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lby4gMvKcLPoozHfg/are-we-in-a-continual-learning-overhang-1"><span>previous post</span></a><span>.&nbsp;Such a system might be quite sample-efficient, and able to learn continually from relatively few real interactions, but only by spending far more computation per interaction on world-model updates, imagined rollouts, planning/search, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26097"><span>replay</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><b><span>3. Other Low-Hanging Fruit</span></b></h1><p><span>Even without brain-like model-based RL, frontier training has not been optimized primarily for extracting maximal information from each real example. Internet-scale text is abundant, and the economically optimal strategy has usually been to train models on ever more unique tokens rather than to squeeze maximal learning out of each example. That leaves a lot of plausible low-hanging fruit. Even just </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16264"><span>naively repeating the same data</span></a><span>&nbsp;for up to 4 epochs can reduce loss on a held out test set almost as well as a single epoch on 4x more data, and yet it is usually still more economical to just use more unique data. But more sophisticated approaches are also possible. Here, I will describe two&nbsp;such approaches.</span></p><h2><span>a. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10055"><b><span>Training Language Models via Neural Cellular Automata</span></b></a></h2><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 61.134582703927485%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d45441d6ba6099b22a8aad7141e387b307fea488c60a5de00ec61e30cb2c204a/jky21cnf7ce5ysz8sxsh" /></figure><p><span>Lee et al. (2026) give a clean example of this. Before ordinary language training, they pre-pre-train a Llama-style transformer on trajectories generated by Neural Cellular Automata (NCA): synthetic 2D grid dynamics where each sequence is produced by a different randomly sampled local update rule. They filter trajectories by </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gzip"><span>gzip</span></a><span>&nbsp;compressibility, using compression ratio as a rough proxy for structural complexity, excluding both trivial patterns and maximally chaotic noise. Successful next-token prediction then requires inferring the rule from context and applying it forward, rather than learning word meanings or memorizing facts. With only ~160M NCA tokens, followed by normal training on web text, math, or code, they get up to ~6% lower perplexity and ~1.4–1.6× faster convergence than training from scratch. The striking result is that NCA pre-pre-training also beats pre-pre-training on ordinary web text from the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.08758"><span>Colossal Clean Crawled Corpus (C4)</span></a><span>, even when the C4 baseline gets 10× more tokens — 1.6B C4 tokens versus ~160M NCA tokens.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 95.81169750755288%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9a65f90e9e48ee5e71e92ebb4fb31d18dabe84e9efbabe51eee6fad2bdf5a863/ml7e4uq7a8ekchnuds89" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 57.06311367069487%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/70c4829b1d49847d8e85ea07e2198b7140db379069d1236156edbc76ca196ec0/smj7rsyqcrihhshlvjp1" /></figure><p><span>Their ablations suggest that much of the transferable benefit lives in the attention layers: when the attention weights are reinitialized, most of the gain disappears. NCA pre-pre-training appears to train the model to infer hidden rules from context, track dependencies over many steps, and apply those inferred rules forward. It instills useful priors for later language learning that can be installed using no “real” data at all, just synthetic processes with the right abstract structure.</span></p><h2><b><span>b. </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15248"><b><span>Synthetic bootstrapped pretraining</span></b></a></h2><p><span>Yang et al. (2025) demonstrate another way to get more value out of a fixed pretraining corpus. Synthetic Bootstrapped Pretraining (SBP) works in three steps: it finds semantically similar document pairs within the corpus, trains a conditional synthesizer model to generate one document from the other, and then applies that model back to the corpus to produce synthetic documents that are mixed into pretraining. Unlike standard synthetic-data distillation, the generator is trained on the pretraining data itself rather than relying on a stronger external teacher model, though their implementation does use an external embedding model to find similar documents. In final pretraining runs matched by token budget, 3B- and 6B-parameter models trained on up to 1T tokens beat a data repetition baseline,&nbsp;and recover up to roughly 60% of the average QA improvement achieved by an oracle with 20x more unique data.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 93.65558912386706%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e519e5d6b27c9fc269a13340d62f211d0ffe91f7e76aa7422f310e841109335b/kuccq0j1kimxq0wq8lai" /></figure><p><span>The authors’ story is that ordinary next-token pretraining leaves some structure in the corpus unused. It treats documents as i.i.d. samples and directly models the correlations among tokens inside each document, but it does not directly model the fact that different documents can instantiate the same underlying idea. SBP adds this missing signal by training on pairs of related documents: the synthesizer has to infer what the first document is about at a more abstract level, and then produce another document built around the same latent concept. The outputs of the synthesizer preserve the topic while changing the angle, genre, specificity, or rhetorical frame.</span></p><p><span>SBP extracts more from the same corpus by spending extra computation on embedding/search, synthesizer training, and synthetic-data generation. It is another example of possible sample-efficiency gains but at the cost of doing more computation per real example.</span></p><h2><b><span>c. Algorithmic progress and the Pareto frontier</span></b></h2><p><span>Both examples contribute to closing the sample-efficiency gap, and both continue the kind of ordinary algorithmic progress we’ve seen over the past few years</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmqzylc0w7wl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmqzylc0w7wl">[13]</a></sup></span><span>. Pareto improvements on both sample and compute efficiency over the simple "scale up unique tokens" baseline do exist. NCA pre-pre-training is one such example: it achieves both lower perplexity and faster convergence than throwing 10× more C4 tokens at the model, so it improves sample and compute efficiency simultaneously.</span></p><p><span>But as the field exhausts these easy wins and pushes toward the algorithmic Pareto frontier, sample-efficiency gains will increasingly need to be paid for with compute. SBP is one such example: it extracts more learning signal from the same corpus, but only by spending non-trivial extra compute on embedding/search, synthesizer training, and synthetic data generation. The picture matches §2: real algorithmic headroom is available, but most of it buys sample efficiency by spending more compute per real example.</span></p><h1><b><span>4. Evolution, optimizers, and hard-coded reward functions in the cortex</span></b></h1><p><span>A common explanation for human sample efficiency is "evolution," but on its own this just relocates the question. Whatever closes the gap has to be encoded in roughly 3 billion base pairs of genome, only a fraction of which specifies anything about the brain at all, and it has to be doing some specific job. A few candidates have already appeared in earlier sections: the rich multimodal representational substrate that lifetime experience accumulates into, structural inductive biases, and the model-based RL machinery itself. That leaves two further candidates worth examining: the optimization algorithm the brain runs, and the hardwired reward functions that shape what lifetime learning ends up optimizing for.</span></p><h2><b><span>a. Optimizer</span></b></h2><p><span>The brain's learning rule is not known, but it almost certainly isn't gradient backpropagation. Backprop has several features that look biologically awkward: feedback signals that behave like a transpose of the forward weights, cleanly separated forward and backward phases, globally coordinated error propagation, and synchronized updates across many layers. None of these have an obvious analog in the brain, and a substantial computational-neuroscience literature is devoted to finding credit-assignment rules that don't require them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref209dzf77qm9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn209dzf77qm9">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>But that doesn't mean the brain uses a much better optimizer than gradient descent. The biologically motivated alternatives are designed around the constraints of wetware: local, noisy, low-precision, asynchronous, with limited global communication. GPUs face none of those constraints, and a learning rule built for biology is unlikely to have an advantage on GPU hardware. Biological plausibility tends to come at a cost. Many need to run the network forward repeatedly before each update, maintain helper networks alongside the main one, or simply scale less well. They're interesting as biology and as starting points for neuromorphic hardware, but on current evidence they don't match backprop with Adam on frontier ML workloads.</span></p><p><span>There may still be some remaining headroom in optimizer design within the gradient-based paradigm. But the design space has been thoroughly searched: there was a long stretch where it seemed like every other ML thesis proposed a new optimizer beating Adam on some narrow benchmark, and yet over a decade later Adam continues to be widely used at the frontier. Recent innovations</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9blwyd777v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9blwyd777v">[15]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;such as Muon do demonstrate some real improvements are still possible, but the optimizer seems unlikely to be the missing ingredient behind the orders-of-magnitude&nbsp;gap in sample efficiency.</span></p><h2><b><span>b. Reward functions</span></b></h2><p><span>One other hypothesis, </span><a href="https://x.com/dwarkesh_sp/status/2049232356998094998"><span>recently articulated</span></a><span>&nbsp;by </span><a href="http://dwarkesh.com/p/adam-marblestone"><span>Adam Marblestone</span></a><a href="http://dwarkesh.com/p/adam-marblestone"><span>&nbsp;on Dwarkesh</span></a><span>, is that human sample efficiency might be explained by genome-encoded reward functions for lifetime RL. The hardwired set of innate drives and primary rewards (hunger, pain, social signals, curiosity, surprise) shapes what lifetime RL ends up optimizing for, and may help the human brain extract more useful learning signal from its lifetime data.</span></p><p><span>The best evidence for this comes from curiosity and novelty rewards. Many </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.02298"><span>classic deep-RL</span></a><span>&nbsp;failures&nbsp;— </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xnvzgQXY9CI"><span>Montezuma's Revenge</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=55S4PJpJ59c"><span>Private Eye</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pslbO6Fddhw"><span>Pitfall</span></a><span>&nbsp;— are sparse-reward games where useful exploration is highly nonrandom. One solution is to bring in pretrained representations and background knowledge, as in §1, and also demonstrated by recent </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sehJYg5Yny9fvpbpt/a-year-late-claude-finally-beats-pokemon"><span>frontier LLMs beating Pokémon Red</span></a><span>. The classic RL response however is using </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.05363"><span>curiosity/novelty rewards</span></a><span>. It is such episodic and lifelong novelty signals that the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.06038"><span>Never Give Up</span></a><span>/</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.13350"><span>Agent57</span></a><span>&nbsp;RL agents leveraged to outperform the standard human benchmark on all 57 Atari games, including </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xnvzgQXY9CI"><span>Montezuma's Revenge</span></a><span>. Such curiosity signals have also been </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.15934"><span>shown to be useful</span></a><span>&nbsp;to prioritize replay data in model-based RL algorithms such as DreamerV3.</span></p><p><span>So reward functions are probably a real part of the sample-efficiency story. But evolution did not arrive at the human reward stack for free. The drives we inherit are the product of hundreds of millions of years of selection, with each organism's lifetime serving as one rollout in an outer optimization process whose objective was reproductive success. To recreate comparable machinery in AI, we need some substitute, via hand design, meta-learning, evolutionary search, learned reward models, or a mixture. Some components such as curiosity drives may be compact and rediscoverable, but the more ecologically tuned and idiosyncratic parts are not a free lunch, and would probably have to be relearned at some computational cost.</span></p><p><span>Byrnes himself&nbsp;seems to be skeptical that reward functions are a large factor. He has written extensively about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oxvnREntu82tffkYW/we-need-a-field-of-reward-function-design"><span>reward function design</span></a><span>&nbsp;as a research direction in the context of alignment, but in his account, the sample-efficiency and general capability gap between human brains and current LLMs mostly comes down to an undiscovered model-based RL paradigm&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oxvnREntu82tffkYW/we-need-a-field-of-reward-function-design?commentId=trcAqvyuknxrPA6d5"><span>rather than to the rewards</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><b><span>5. Size matters</span></b></h1><h2><b><span>a. Data efficiency and scaling laws</span></b></h2><p><span>There’s a further factor that may help explain the brain’s sample efficiency: its sheer size. The human brain is estimated to have around ~150 trillion synapses.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkc6r8ckhv2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkc6r8ckhv2">[16]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;A naive comparison to the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://x.com/Yuchenj_UW/status/2037387996694200509"><span>rumored size</span></a><span>&nbsp;of one of the largest frontier models,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6kheve8blkq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6kheve8blkq">[17]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Claude Mythos, equating synapse with parameter count,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1telqtpxbml"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1telqtpxbml">[18]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;would give the brain roughly a 15x size advantage. This matters because larger models generally need less data to reach a given target loss. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.15556"><span>Hoffmann et al.'s (2022)</span></a><span>&nbsp;"Chinchilla" paper, replicated and corrected by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.10102"><span>Besiroglu et al. (2024)</span></a><span>, fits final pretraining loss as a function of parameter count N and training tokens D:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>The Chinchilla approach assumes compute is the binding constraint and asks how to split it between parameters and data. Biology faced a different binding constraint: data, capped by lifespan, with parameter count more easily scaled at metabolic cost. The result is that the brain was pushed to a wildly off-Chinchilla operating point: enormous N, tiny D. That allocation is a terrible way to minimize loss given a fixed total compute budget. But under a lifespan-limited data budget, the best strategy is to spend metabolic resources on representational capacity, built-in structure, and learning mechanisms that squeeze more value out of each observation. The brain looks sample-efficient in part because it is an extremely sparse, massively over-parameterized network running a learning procedure tuned for exactly that regime.</span></p><h2><b><span>b. Why it matters in particular in the case of self-driving cars</span></b></h2><p><span>The size factor&nbsp;is probably a dominant driver of apparent data inefficiency in the self-driving case. Waymo has now driven </span><a href="https://waymo.com/blog/2026/02/the-waymo-world-model-a-new-frontier-for-autonomous-driving-simulation/"><span>~200 million autonomous miles on public roads</span></a><span>, and Tesla's customer fleet has </span><a href="https://x.com/Tesla/status/2050991662076477504"><span>recently reached 10B miles driven</span></a><span>&nbsp;with FSD supervised, many human lifetimes' worth of driving experience. Not only that, both companies actually already run something close to the model-based-RL story sketched in §2, at least during training. Waymo's recently announced </span><a href="https://waymo.com/blog/2026/02/the-waymo-world-model-a-new-frontier-for-autonomous-driving-simulation/"><span>World Model</span></a><span>&nbsp;is a Genie-3-derived generative simulator that produces sensor-aligned camera and lidar observations, which Waymo uses to train its Driver on billions of simulated miles. Tesla </span><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2034534654506840540"><span>similarly trains FSD inside its own world model</span></a><span>&nbsp;generated from fleet video. Whatever data efficiency is being left on the table here, it is not the absence of a learned world model to train inside.</span></p><p><span>What is probably a binding constraint is parameter count of the policy that ships on the car. The world models themselves can be large and run in datacenters during training, but the network that actually steers the vehicle has to fit on the vehicle. Frontier LLMs are typically served on something like&nbsp;8×H200 (~1,128 GB of HBM); Tesla's deployed Hardware 4, by contrast, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_Autopilot_hardware#Hardware_4"><span>has 16 GB</span></a><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_Autopilot_hardware#Hardware_4"><span>&nbsp;of RAM</span></a><span>. That is room for roughly 32B 4-bit parameters before any allowance for activations or the rest of the perception and planning stack. By naive comparison of the raw parameter count to synapses, that puts the size of the onboard model&nbsp;at more than three orders of magnitude below the brain of the human it is supposed to eventually outperform.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9ozeed7eob5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9ozeed7eob5">[19]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>If self-driving follows a Chinchilla-style scaling law, this is exactly backwards from the human regime: a small, hardware-fixed N forced to compensate with very large D. For any given onboard compute platform, there is a ceiling on the reliability Tesla and Waymo can achieve. More miles, better architectures, and better simulators push the system toward that ceiling, and are responsible for some of the impressive improvements in self-driving capability in recent years. But truly solving self-driving might still require a hardware upgrade, not just more data or better algorithms.</span></p><h1><b><span>6. Implications</span></b></h1><h2><b><span>a. On brain in a box in a basement</span></b></h2><p><span>While Byrnes’ broader brain in a box in a basement thesis rests on several other premises about neuroscience (e.g. cortical uniformity) and algorithmic paradigms that are beyond the scope of this post, the brain’s sample efficiency does not provide good evidence for the existence of a major undiscovered algorithmic regime achieving orders of magnitude gains in both compute and sample efficiency simultaneously.</span></p><p><span>He may have ideas for much better model-based RL algorithms that would be infohazardous to share publicly, but the factors surveyed in sections 1-5 collectively look adequate to explain the gap. None of these point toward consumer-hardware training budgets and several point the other way. </span></p><p><span>Existing model-based RL algorithms do demonstrate impressive sample efficiency, but in tightly bounded environments. Scaling that style of algorithm into a system that learns language, physics, social cognition, and tool use&nbsp;within a consumer-hardware training budget is a tall order. The world is large and complex. And while the core of intelligence may be simple, and the code to train an AGI may well fit in a few hundred lines of PyTorch code, the compute budget to train such an AGI appears unlikely to be within at least 3 orders of magnitude&nbsp;of the best consumer GPU today.</span></p><p><span>One important implication is that compute governance is unlikely to become irrelevant by the discovery of much more compute-efficient algorithms. This applies most directly to training compute. I’m a lot less confident about inference. A trained model can be much smaller than the system that produced it, and there are strong economic incentives to overtrain in the opposite direction from the brain's regime, yielding small, deployable policies, with little spare capacity. So an AGI-level model might plausibly fit on a consumer GPU at inference time, even if its training run did not. Such a deployed policy would still likely be unable to bootstrap itself dramatically further on the same hardware, since the world-model updates, replay, and planning needed for serious open-ended continual learning remain compute-intensive.</span></p><p><span>That said, Byrnes is right that model-based RL is a missing piece in the current frontier-LLM stack, and it matters most in the regimes where current RLVR pipelines struggle: tasks where rewards aren't cheaply verifiable, where real interaction data is expensive, and where the agent needs to keep learning after deployment. The alignment community should pay more attention to this research direction. The safety properties of a system that plans and learns inside a learned world model are meaningfully different from those of one that doesn't, and if model-based RL architectures end up on the critical path to transformative AI, we want the conceptual groundwork in place well before they are deployed at scale. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xw8P8H4TRaTQHJnoP/reward-function-design-a-starter-pack"><span>Reward function design</span></a><span>&nbsp;appears to be one important and promising research direction.</span></p><h2><b><span>b. On sample efficiency being an unsolved research problem</span></b></h2><p><span>The evidence also does not support Bouchard's opposite conclusion that current deep learning is missing some truly major ingredient, and that AGI is therefore far away. The most dramatic sample-efficiency comparisons are often structurally unfair, pitting pretrained humans against randomly initialized networks, text-only models against children embedded in a rich multimodal stream, or small deployed policies against human brains with vastly larger effective capacity. Additionally, frontier systems have not been under strong economic pressure to maximize sample efficiency. Much of the apparent sample inefficiency of deep learning is a byproduct of how we train, deploy, and compare these systems.</span></p><p><span>Once these factors are pulled apart, the remaining gap largely comes down to a few missing algorithmic pieces, such as world models, reward models, replay, synthetic data, and continual learning. We have a rough idea of what the remedies look like, and the remaining bottleneck appears to be mostly about scaling and figuring out how to integrate these missing components in open-ended domains, not about finding some wholly new paradigm.</span></p><p><span>Because these components generally cost compute to integrate and scale, progress is likely to remain uneven. That unevenness may carry some safety upside, such as giving us superhuman coders well before agents with persistent episodic memory. But none of it is a durable barrier to automation or to dangerous capabilities. Deep learning can remain less sample-efficient than humans and still be extremely disruptive.</span></p><p><span>Even if the de facto data efficiency stays 100x below humans indefinitely, this does not prevent rapid job automation. Once a model is trained, the marginal cost of a second instance is just the compute to run it, which can easily sit well below the salary of the human it replaces. Human data efficiency only preserves a defensible economic niche where the cost of automating the work, including data collection, training, and inference, exceeds the wage bill being displaced, and the task requires adapting to new situations faster than models can be trained to do the work from scratch, or develop rich enough representations to learn to perform the work in-context from more limited samples.</span></p><h1><b><span>Conclusion</span></b></h1><p><span>Most of the apparent sample-efficiency gap dissolves under scrutiny. It comes from pitting pretrained humans against from-scratch networks, text-only models against children embedded in a multimodal stream, and small deployed policies against much larger brains. What remains can mostly be closed through familiar means like reward functions, world models, replay, synthetic data, multimodal training, larger models, and better continual learning. These typically pay for sample efficiency with compute. So the gap gives us neither reason to expect AGI trained on consumer hardware, nor reason to think deep learning is missing some major ingredient.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><b><span>Appendix: How Much Multimodal Data Does a Child Actually Receive?</span></b></h1><p><span>One version of this estimate comes from Yann LeCun's </span><a href="https://cmsa.fas.harvard.edu/media/lecun-20240328-harvard_reduced.pdf#page=20"><span>2024 Harvard slides</span></a><span>: 2 million optic nerve fibers × 10 bytes/sec × 16,000 wake hours over 4 years ≈ 1.15 PB of visual data, against 10¹³ tokens × 2 bytes ≈ 20 TB of text, or a ~57× advantage to a 4-year-old. Extended to age 12 with the same assumptions, the visual figure scales to ~3.45 PB and the gap widens to ~170×. Three of the estimates in this calculation deserve scrutiny.</span></p><p><b><span>Per-fiber bandwidth.</span></b><span>&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0960982206016393"><span>Koch et al. (2006)</span></a><span>&nbsp;(</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Eg5AEMhGdyyKRWmZW/is-gpt-3-already-sample-efficient?commentId=LGkPqRuWhEe8c9Ajs"><span>h/t Byrnes</span></a><span>) measure information rates of guinea-pig retinal ganglion cells under naturalistic stimuli and, assuming roughly independent fibers, estimate the human retina's aggregate output at ~10 Mbit/s from ~1M ganglion cells, i.e. ~10 bits/s/fiber on average, not 10 bytes/s.</span></p><p><b><span>Fiber count and binocular redundancy.</span></b><span>&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41433-024-03137-7"><span>Pawar et al. 2024</span></a><span>&nbsp;puts the human optic nerve at ~1M axons per eye, so 2 × 10⁶ is the bilateral total. But the two eyes carry heavily overlapping fields, so I will use a 1.2x unique information multiplier across both eyes, not 2×.</span></p><p><b><span>Frontier corpus size.</span></b><span>&nbsp;2026 leading open model training corpora are now up to 1.5-4 × 10¹³ tokens (</span><a href="https://github.com/meta-llama/llama-models/blob/main/models/llama3_1/MODEL_CARD.md"><span>Llama 3.1: 15T</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437v1"><span>DeepSeek-V3: 14.8T</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09388v1"><span>Qwen 3: 36T</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/meta-llama/Llama-4-Scout-17B-16E"><span>Llama 4 Scout: 40T</span></a><span>), instead of LeCun's 10¹³.</span></p><p><br /><span>For the 12-year extrapolation below, I round up to 2 × 10</span><sup><span>8</span></sup><span>&nbsp;waking seconds, rather than the 1.73 × 10</span><sup><span>8</span></sup><span> seconds obtained by naively tripling LeCun’s 4-year wake-time assumption, to account for children being awake more hours per day as they get older.</span><br /></p><h3><b><span>Visual input over 12 years</span></b></h3><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LeCun's 2024 figure </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Corrected</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Optic nerve fibers (per eye)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1 × 10⁶</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~1 × 10⁶</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Bandwidth per fiber</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>80 bits/s</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~10 bits/s</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Binocular adjustment</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2x</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.2×</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Effective info rate</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.6 × 10⁸ bits/s</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~1.2 × 10⁷ bits/s</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Waking seconds, ages 0–12</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~2 × 10⁸</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~2 × 10⁸</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 50px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Total over 12 years</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~3.2 × 10</span><sup><span>16</span></sup><span> bits (~4 PB)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~2.4 × 10</span><sup><span>15</span></sup><span> bits (~300 TB)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h3><span>Frontier text corpus, 2026</span></h3><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LeCun's 2024 figure</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2026 open frontier</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Tokens</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>10¹³</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://huggingface.co/meta-llama/Llama-4-Scout-17B-16E"><span>4 × 10¹³</span></a></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Bits/token</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>16 (storage)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>16 storage / ~4 entropy*</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Total, raw storage</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.6 × 10</span><sup><span>14</span></sup><span> bits (~20 TB)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>6.4 × 10</span><sup><span>14</span></sup><span> bits (~80 TB) </span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Total, info content</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~8 × 10¹³ bits</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~1.6 × 10</span><sup><span>14</span></sup><span> bits</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><h3><span>Visual / text ratios at age 12</span></h3><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Visual / text</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LeCun's numbers throughout</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>~170×</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LeCun visual + 2026 frontier text</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>~43×</span></b><span>&nbsp;</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Koch-grounded retina + 2026 raw storage</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>~4×</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Koch-grounded retina + 2026 info content</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>~15×</span></b></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>*Assuming tokenizer with 65k vocab, </span><a href="https://help.openai.com/en/articles/4936856-what-are-tokens-and-how-to-count-them"><span>1 token ≈ 4 characters</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://mattmahoney.net/dc///entropy1.html"><span>1 bit of entropy per character</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Best guess: in information-theoretic terms, raw retinal output to a 12-year-old exceeds a 2026-frontier text corpus by roughly 15×. Adding audio, touch, proprioception, and vestibular streams might add another ~2×. So the multimodal child's lifetime stream sits around one OOM above frontier text corpora in retinal-output informational bits, after the substantial compression already implicit in Koch et al., but before accounting for additional longer-timescale redundancy in visual experience. The informational gap appears to be roughly within one OOM.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9lyzj9lpm6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9lyzj9lpm6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-019-1724-z"><span>AlphaStar’s paper</span></a><span>&nbsp;(</span><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/research/alphastar/AlphaStar_unformatted.pdf"><span>no paywall</span></a><span>) reports supervised pretraining on 971,000 human games. Since it describes StarCraft II games as lasting roughly 10 minutes, this corresponds to 971,000×10 minutes ≈18.5 replay-years of human games.</span></p><p><span>It does not directly report self-play game-years. However, its Methods state that the league used 12 actor-learner setups, trained over 44 days, each learner processing about 50,000 agent steps/s, with received data replayed twice; interpreting this as about 25,000 newly generated agent steps/s per setup, and using Extended Data Fig. 2’s average agent-step interval of about 369 ms, gives 12×25,000×0.369×44×86,400≈4.2×1011&nbsp;seconds of generated agent experience, or about 13,300 game-years.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc2uzas6fjlg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc2uzas6fjlg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“Instead, my guess (based largely on lots of opinions about exactly what computations the human brain is doing and how) is that human-level human-speed AGI will require not a datacenter, but rather something like one consumer gaming GPU—and not just for inference, but even for training from scratch.” </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yew6zFWAKG4AGs3Wk/foom-and-doom-1-brain-in-a-box-in-a-basement"><span>(Byrnes 2025)</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbg4g66a8xct"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbg4g66a8xct">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28418456/"><span>Gilkerson et al. (2017)</span></a><span>&nbsp;estimate roughly a few million adult words/year for 2–48-month-olds; a naive extrapolation to age 12 gives an order of magnitude of tens of millions of words; the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08165"><span>BabyLM challenge</span></a><span>&nbsp;rounds to 100M by age 13.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm3ome6f3u8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm3ome6f3u8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A common hypothesis is that humans are highly sample efficient because they receive curated curricula. I doubt this is an important factor. Most of what a child learns is picked up with nothing resembling a curriculum. Adult language learners also reach fluency faster via immersion than classroom instruction. And in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08165"><span>BabyLM Challenge</span></a><span>, strategies relying on curriculum learning showed little improvement.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmdl00bx003a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmdl00bx003a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Given the text-dominated training mix and objective of next-token prediction, even 2022-era LMs are already </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/htrZrxduciZ5QaCjw/language-models-seem-to-be-much-better-than-humans-at-next"><span>better than humans at it</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneqbcuzdjhh7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeqbcuzdjhh7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.07677"><span>von Oswald et al. (2023)</span></a><span>&nbsp;hypothesized that self-attention transforms activations in a way approximately equivalent to gradient descent on an implicit loss, though this specific mechanistic claim </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08540"><span>is contested</span></a><span>. End-to-End Test-Time Training&nbsp;</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23675"><span>(Tandon et al. 2025)</span></a><span>, though, has shown that with a pre-training method leveraging gradient-of-gradients, a similar effect to self-attention in-context learning can also be achieved directly via test-time gradient descent.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn64lwn3zjt6f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref64lwn3zjt6f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippocampal_replay"><span>Hippocampal replay</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnls7nvjol7pc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefls7nvjol7pc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See also LeCun, </span><a href="https://openreview.net/pdf?id=BZ5a1r-kVsf"><span>A Path Towards Autonomous Machine Intelligence (2022)</span></a><span>, for a case along similar lines.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna6h12tylufk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa6h12tylufk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mRwJce3npmzbKfxws/efficientzero-how-it-works"><span>here</span></a><span>&nbsp;for a LW explainer of the original EfficientZero algorithm.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5lfh86vv2ja"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5lfh86vv2ja">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>EZ-V2 beats the human score on 15 of 26 games (58%). On games where model-based RL pulls ahead it often pulls far ahead — </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WW17PNeTSHU"><span>Asterix</span></a><span>&nbsp;62k vs 8.5k, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jEqOnAJQiqY"><span>Crazy Climber</span></a><span>&nbsp;112k vs 36k, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5GwoSr12kxY"><span>Demon Attack</span></a><span>&nbsp;23k vs 2k &nbsp;— and on games where it struggles it struggles catastrophically: </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=55S4PJpJ59c"><span>Private Eye</span></a><span>&nbsp;100 vs ~70,000, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ir1t57ItCpw"><span>Seaquest</span></a><span>&nbsp;2k vs 42k, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bOZ7TIx5Zv8"><span>Alien</span></a><span>&nbsp;1.5k vs 7.1k. The normalized mean is dragged up by the blowouts. On 9 of the 26 games (~35%) — </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bOZ7TIx5Zv8"><span>Alien</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QNkq6NXijX8"><span>Amidar</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9X4_xy7rC1A"><span>BattleZone</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AYjZ88RAGN8"><span>Freeway</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J2oSbAbcOPg"><span>Frostbite</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1YAmdDE3ivk"><span>Hero</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CYjSI4Pnh7M"><span>Ms. Pac-Man</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=55S4PJpJ59c"><span>Private Eye</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ir1t57ItCpw"><span>Seaquest</span></a><span>&nbsp;— the human baseline still beats every deep RL algorithm in the table. These are, fairly consistently, games requiring long-horizon credit assignment under sparse reward (</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=55S4PJpJ59c"><span>Private Eye</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ir1t57ItCpw"><span>Seaquest</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J2oSbAbcOPg"><span>Frostbite</span></a><span>), systematic exploration of large maze-like state spaces (</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bOZ7TIx5Zv8"><span>Alien</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QNkq6NXijX8"><span>Amidar</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CYjSI4Pnh7M"><span>Ms. Pac-Man</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1YAmdDE3ivk"><span>Hero</span></a><span>), or patient timing against a slow environment (</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AYjZ88RAGN8"><span>Freeway</span></a><span>). The comfortable wins for deep RL are mostly reactive arcade games with dense reward signals.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyj6gplx2sll"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyj6gplx2sll">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>LLMs do learn an implicit world model during pre-training, but it isn't structured for the kind of use model-based RL make of one. It can't be cleanly queried during training to generate imagined rollouts, and post-training further entangles its representations with those of the policy and persona. Chain-of-thought training may be a partial workaround during inference. By generating intermediate text, the model effectively queries its own implicit world model, recovering some of the benefits of model-based&nbsp;planning.</span></p><p><span>Architectures in which the world model is kept separate from the policy and not updated by RL gradients, but instead trained only by self-supervised learning, might have better safety properties.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne7yrhxiifoa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe7yrhxiifoa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The main domain “narrow world model” simulations have been applied to is to simulate user interactions. Public examples include Google/DeepMind’s </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-08866-7"><span>AMIE</span></a><span>&nbsp;medical self-play, Moonshot’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20534v2"><span>Kimi K2 agentic data pipeline</span></a><span>&nbsp;with synthetic user personas and tool-use trajectories, and Salesforce’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.03601"><span>APIGen-MT</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmqzylc0w7wl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmqzylc0w7wl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See also Byrnes’ </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sGNFtWbXiLJg2hLzK/the-nature-of-llm-algorithmic-progress-v2"><span>The nature of LLM algorithmic progress (v2)</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn209dzf77qm9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref209dzf77qm9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Candidate biologically plausible credit-assignment schemes include </span><i><span>predictive coding </span></i><span>(</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-017-0057-5"><span>Whittington &amp; Bogacz, 2017</span></a><span>; see also Millidge et al. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.04182"><span>2020</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.12979"><span>2021</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02629"><span>2022</span></a><span>&nbsp;and </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/2023-03-30-Thoughts-on-future-of-PC/"><span>Millidge 2023</span></a><span>&nbsp;for an informal retrospective); </span><i><span>equilibrium propagation</span></i><span>&nbsp;(</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.05179"><span>Scellier &amp; Bengio, 2017</span></a><span>); and </span><i><span>target propagation</span></i><span>&nbsp;(</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1407.7906"><span>Bengio, 2014</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1412.7525"><span>Lee et al., 2015</span></a><span>). These relax some of backprop's biological implausibilities, but usually pay with settling dynamics, extra inverse machinery, or weaker scaling. The literature is therefore better read as evidence for possible biological credit assignment than for a GPU-superior optimizer.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9blwyd777v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9blwyd777v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Recent examples of newer more sample efficient optimizers include </span><a href="https://kellerjordan.github.io/posts/muon/"><span>Muon (Jordan, 2024)</span></a><span>, which orthogonalizes gradient updates via a Newton-Schulz iteration, and M3 (Behrouz et al., 2025), the optimizer described in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24695"><span>Nested Learning paper</span></a><span>&nbsp;I discussed in my </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lby4gMvKcLPoozHfg/are-we-in-a-continual-learning-overhang-1"><span>continual learning post</span></a><span>, which builds on Muon with multi-scale momentum and Adam-like normalization, improving effective sample efficiency at the cost of more memory and compute per step.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkc6r8ckhv2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkc6r8ckhv2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There’s some uncertainty about the true number in the literature, so relying on the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_animals_by_number_of_neurons"><span>Wikipedia consensus</span></a><span>. The number is </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/35081509"><span>probably higher during infancy</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6kheve8blkq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6kheve8blkq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/veFMEzDDyWaer2Sms/sanity-checking-incompressible-knowledge-probes"><span>here</span></a><span>&nbsp;for some additional parameter count estimates of frontier models, which make me think that the 10T estimate for Mythos is quite plausible.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1telqtpxbml"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1telqtpxbml">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The parameter to synapse equivalence in terms of useful computation is highly uncertain. The synapse count might be an underestimate for how many parameters equivalent the brain really has. </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0896627321005018"><span>Beniaguev et al. (2021)</span></a><span>&nbsp;found that a detailed biophysical model of a cortical pyramidal neuron was well approximated by a temporally convolutional deep neural network with 5 to 8 layers, suggesting that treating each biological neuron as a simple artificial neuron might miss real computation happening within dendritic trees. That said, this result has important caveats (see this </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/h7Rj8Y8YWZccYMy5J/it-takes-5-layers-and-1000-artificial-neurons-to-simulate-a"><span>EAF discussion</span></a><span>&nbsp;for more). The study did not run the reverse comparison, so we do not know whether comparable overhead applies going from artificial to biological. The per-neuron overhead might also not scale linearly for larger networks. Furthermore, much of the simulated complexity may not be functionally useful. As Byrnes argues (</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/h7Rj8Y8YWZccYMy5J/it-takes-5-layers-and-1000-artificial-neurons-to-simulate-a?commentId=Siqqw5kYHSmMkLKWZ"><span>1</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/PTkd8nazvH9HQpwP8/building-brain-inspired-agi-is-infinitely-easier-than"><span>2</span></a><span>), biological systems are typically full of dynamics that are not load-bearing for the system's useful function, much like how a real transistor is </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20170830052605/http://csit-sun.pub.ro/courses/vlsi/VLSI_Darmstad/www.microelectronic.e-technik.tu-darmstadt.de/lectures/winter/vlsi/vorlesung_pdf/chap04.pdf"><span>described by a 22-parameter physics model</span></a><span>, even though its useful computational function is just an ON/OFF switch. I use a 1-to-1 synapse-to-parameter equivalence as a probably conservative baseline, but the real magnitude is unclear.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9ozeed7eob5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9ozeed7eob5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This three-OOM figure should be read loosely. CNN-style weight sharing lets the onboard net avoid replicating visual feature detectors across space the way the visual cortex must, shrinking the effective gap.
